**Where a goal sits** (Dorian, 2026-10-01). The project charter is this document’s Goal and Constraints, and it changes only by an amendment he approves. Objectives are the sections under it. A goal is a task-sized or design-sized outcome under one objective, written as a goal brief, and its items are the plan inside it. Writing a goal never changes the charter. A goal can only narrow it: fewer paths, a smaller budget, stricter tests. A goal that turns out to need the charter loosened stops and goes to him as an amendment, decided before the goal runs again. Goals come from the open work under each objective, the session handoff and the project’s Jira label. The agent drafts goal briefs, a council ranks them, and Dorian picks one and approves its pull request. Later, when a goal finishes, the agent proposes the next one from the same sources.

Merges already need no human outside `CODEOWNERS` paths (DR-113). What goal mode removes is the per-item ask: Dorian approving each DESIGN.md amendment and picking the next item. For the first goals he still approves two things per goal: the goal folder (brief, plan and tests, one pull request) and the goal branch's pull request into `main`, through GitHub's own code-owner review at its head (GO-003.7). The bot merges once that approval exists. After two or three goals that finish clean and are audited, those two approvals become one by amending this section, and only then may the bot merge a goal branch into `main` without his review at its head.

Not taken, on the council's advice: design-loop convergence as approval (not before the clean goals above), risk tiers the agent assigns itself (an enforced path list and fixed action types instead), dependency-aware replanning (the plan's order is the dependency order, which keeps the static-workflow constraint), and a general council framework (GO-005's action types instead).

### GO-001 — A goal is a brief Dorian approved, under one objective, and the approval binds to one tree

**Status:** done. Merged to `main` in `b078776` (PR #100, head `d7c8a83`) on 1 October 2026 by `moxywolf-agent[bot]` after Dorian's approving review at the head, recorded by `record-release` as `agent_merge_autonomous` (authority DR-113). Review `20261001-172256-450b5df-vqxfhb5v` (codex/gpt-6-astra, `fixes_verified`, 3 rounds, 6/6, coverage `checked`): round 1 found eight blockers, round 2 verified six, round 3 the last two. One reading came from the review and is kept: an approved goal never changes, so `verify` accepts only the PR that adds `goals/<id>/`.

**Links introduced:** the approval-to-goal link. A reader of a run record assumes the brief, plan and tests it names are the ones Dorian approved. That holds because the run record carries the git tree hash of `goals/<id>/` at the commit his approving review names, and the runner refuses to start or continue when `main`'s copy differs.

1. A goal is `goals/<id>/`: `GOAL.md`, `PLAN.md`, `tests/` and `holdout.sha256`. `GOAL.md`, the goal brief, has these sections, each required and non-empty: Serves (the heading of the one DESIGN.md objective this goal works under), Outcome, Non-goals, Scenarios (plain-language "given / then" lines, plus at least one "must never happen" line), Goal tests (test IDs, each marked `outcome` or `invariant`, see GO-002), Allowed paths (globs), Spend cap (US dollars), Provider budgets (one dollar amount per metered provider the run may call, summing to no more than the Spend cap), Max calls (for transports with no metered key, see GO-004), Max items, Max review rounds per item, Stop conditions, Pre-mortem (ways the tests could pass while the goal fails). `PLAN.md` lists the items in run order, each with its own acceptance criteria.
2. `plugins/gstack-execution/scripts/goal_brief.py check <goal dir>` exits 0 only when every section and file in criterion 1 is present and parses, Serves names an objective heading that exists in `main`’s DESIGN.md, Allowed paths excludes every `CODEOWNERS` path, Max items is between 1 and 10 and the plan lists no more than that, Max review rounds is between 1 and 3, Spend cap and Max calls are positive, and every Provider budget is a finite positive number and together they sum to no more than the Spend cap. It names each failure, prints how many sections and files it examined, and examining none fails.
3. The approval is Dorian's approving GitHub review of the pull request that adds `goals/<id>/`. Before a run starts, `goal_brief.py verify <id>` reads that review through the GitHub API and requires: reviewer `dorianatmoxywolf`, state `APPROVED`, `commit_id` equal to the pull request's head, the pull request merged, and the tree hash of `goals/<id>/` at that head equal to `main`'s. The run record stores the PR number, review ID, head and tree hash.
4. `goals/`, `goal-runs/` and `goal_brief.py` join `CODEOWNERS` and the gate-script list in *Constraints*, and `.github/test_codeowners.py` expects them. `goal_runner.py` (GO-003) and `goal_envelope.py` (GO-004) join both in the item that creates each, because the check fails on a gate path that doesn't exist yet.
5. A brief never loosens the charter. The bounds in criterion 2 are the charter’s limits as code, and a brief outside them is refused, not negotiated. When a run needs something only a change to *Constraints* would allow, it stops with outcome `stopped` and names the constraint, and the change goes to Dorian as a DESIGN.md amendment before any goal relies on it.
6. A test covers: a valid goal folder; each missing section or file; a Serves line naming no objective; an Allowed path that matches a `CODEOWNERS` path; Max items 0 and 11; Max review rounds 4; a review by someone else, one in state `COMMENTED`, one at an older commit, and a `main` tree that differs from the approved one, each refused by `verify`.

### GO-002 — Goal tests are written by someone other than the builder, and the holdout stays out of the builder's reach

**Status:** done. Merged to `main` in `3141db2` (PR #101, head `8457636`) on 1 October 2026 by `moxywolf-agent[bot]` after Dorian's approving review at the head, recorded by `record-release` as `agent_merge_autonomous` (authority DR-113). Review `20261001-193056-c8710c6-jw8onu03` (codex) ended `rounds_exhausted` on one finding, the inventory refusing module-scope repository imports; it was fixed as the reviewer proposed and, on Dorian's approval, a fresh review `20261001-200338-6fd82a0-iq8pcghi` found one more (module fixtures skipped by baseline) and ended `fixes_verified` in 2 rounds. Criteria 3, 5 and 6 were re-homed on 2026-10-01: the holdout job's sandbox, the mid-run stop and the builder-family refusal are run-time behaviour of the runner and the `goal-holdout` workflow, so they're built and tested in GO-003 (criteria 3, 5 and 9 there), not claimed here.

**Links introduced:** the test-to-intent link. A reader assumes a passing goal test means the brief's scenario happened. That holds only as far as the test encodes the scenario, which is why each test names its scenario and a different model translates it back to plain English for Dorian.

1. Goal tests live in `goals/<id>/tests/` as unittest methods, named in the brief as `tests/<file>.py::<Class>.<method>`. Every test file carries a `# drafted-by: <family>/<model>` line and one family drafts them all. `goal_brief.py check` refuses a missing line, mixed families, or a brief test ID that names no existing method. The builder is never that family (GO-003.3). A goal test never imports the candidate or reads its checkout: it runs the candidate as a separate program from the checkout `GOAL_CANDIDATE` names and judges what it prints or writes elsewhere, so candidate code never shares the test's interpreter and can't reach its verdict. `check` refuses a test file that needs the repository to load, and the harness reports `not_run` for a test that opens anything in the candidate's checkout.
2. Each test is marked `outcome` or `invariant`. Before the goal pull request can merge, `goal_brief.py baseline <goal dir>` runs them against a checkout of `origin/main`, never the working tree, inside the `goal-tests` container (GO-003.5), so a test that can't run there fails here too: every `outcome` test must fail and every `invariant` test must pass. A test on the wrong side is named and fails the check. It reports how many tests it examined, and examining none fails.
3. The holdout is at least one test stored as the secret `GOAL_<ID>_HOLDOUT` of a GitHub environment `goal-holdout` whose deployment branches are `main` only, never in the repository, the vault or Taskade, and set by Dorian. A workflow running on any other ref, including one pushed to an item branch, can't read it. `goals/<id>/holdout.sha256` records its hash (GO-001.2 checks its form). The `moxywolf-agent` app holds no secrets permission, recorded in `docs/evidence/go-002-app-permissions-2026-10-01.md`. The job that reads it, and its sandbox, are GO-003.5.
4. Each test's docstring carries a `Scenario:` line that is one of the brief's Scenarios, and `check` refuses one that doesn't. The goal pull request's description carries a `## Plain-English reading` section that covers every goal test ID and names its reader on a `Read by: <family>/<model>` line, a family other than the drafter's. `goal_brief.py verify` refuses a PR without it. The builder is neither family (GO-003.3).
5. A test that looks wrong goes back to Dorian as a new goal folder, never a builder fix (GO-001: an approved goal never changes). Stopping a run when `goals/<id>/` changes is GO-003's.
6. A test covers: an `outcome` test that passes on `main` and an `invariant` test that fails there (both refused); a baseline over no tests; a test ID naming no method; a test without a brief Scenario; a test file without `drafted-by`; two drafting families; a PR with no plain-English reading, one that misses a test, one with no reader, and one read by the drafting family.
7. A Node candidate runs too (amended 2026-10-05). The goal sandbox is one image for every governed repository, pinned by digest in `goal_checks.IMAGE`: Playwright's image (1.64.1, Ubuntu noble), which carries Node 24, the harness's Python 3.12 and Playwright's browsers (amended 2026-10-25). A candidate with a `package-lock.json` has its dependencies installed first, in a container of their own that has the network and nothing else. It gets only `package.json`, `package-lock.json` and `.npmrc`, never the checkout, a secret or a goal test, and runs `npm ci --ignore-scripts`, so no package runs code while it installs. The installed folder is mounted read-only at `/deps` in the sandbox, which still has no network. The candidate runs from a copy on the sandbox's `/tmp` with `node_modules` linked to it, because a build writes into its own folder. A failed install fails the check and names why. A build that fetches from the network (`next/font/google` downloading a font, say) fails in the sandbox by design: the repository vendors what it needs, or its goal tests don't run that build. A goal test may drive a page in a headless browser: the image's browsers sit at `PLAYWRIGHT_BROWSERS_PATH`, which the test's otherwise empty environment keeps, and the candidate's `@playwright/test` must match the image's version (amended 2026-10-25, Dorian's decision on goal wfa-sources-v1). Database tests are out of scope. A test covers a Node candidate built offline from its locked dependencies, a candidate with no lockfile installing nothing, and the install container getting only the three files.
8. The holdout needs no secret (amended 2026-10-05, Dorian's choice: a manual step per goal, or one key per repository or organization, defeats the point). `goal_new.py holdout` writes it to `~/.goal-holdouts/<id>.py` on the machine the run uses, outside every repository, and only its SHA-256 goes into the goal folder Dorian approves. It stays there through the build. When the goal tests pass, `goal_run.py finalize` reads it with code, refuses it unless it matches the approved hash, and commits it as `goal-runs/<id>/holdout.py` beside the run record, so it reaches the repository only in the goal pull request. `goal-holdout` reads it from that pull request's merge candidate, checks the hash again, and deletes the file from the candidate before the candidate runs, so the code under test can't open it. The secret `GOAL_<ID>_HOLDOUT` still works where one is set. The trade, accepted: the holdout sits on a machine the building agent can reach for the whole run, so only its instructions keep it from reading it. The hash check means a swapped holdout fails loudly. A test covers the finalize step carrying a matching holdout and refusing a missing or swapped one, the envelope allowing the finalize commit with its holdout and nothing else, and `goal-holdout` running the carried holdout with the file gone before the candidate runs.
9. A pnpm app in a subfolder runs too (amended 2026-10-24, from goal `wfa-web-v1` in workforce-automation, whose tests run `pnpm --dir web install --frozen-lockfile --offline` and the build: the sandbox image has no pnpm and the install read only a root `package-lock.json`, so those tests could never pass however good the app). Every folder one or two levels below the root with its own `pnpm-lock.yaml` is a pnpm project. Each must pin pnpm exactly in its `package.json` `packageManager`, and all must pin the same version, or the check refuses and names the folder. Its packages install in the same networked install container, from that folder's `package.json`, `pnpm-lock.yaml`, `.npmrc` and `pnpm-workspace.yaml` only, through corepack with `--frozen-lockfile --ignore-scripts`, hoisted. In the sandbox the folder's `node_modules` is copied into the candidate's copy on `/tmp`, not linked, because pnpm recreates a `node_modules` it reaches through a symlink and `/deps` is read-only, and a `pnpm` on `PATH` runs the pinned version from the copied corepack cache with the network off. So the goal test's own `pnpm install --offline` finds everything current and changes nothing. A root-level pnpm workspace is still `goal_local`'s (GO-010.2). Tests cover a pnpm app in `web/` with no root `package.json` installing and building offline in the sandbox, the pin rules, the install container getting that folder's manifest files only, and the copied `node_modules` and the pinned `pnpm` in the sandbox.

### GO-003 — `/gstack-goal` runs the plan onto a goal branch, and `main` moves once

**Status:** done. Criteria 1, 3, 4 and 8 merged in PR #104 (`e297a22`), criterion 5 and the sealed goal tests in PR #105 (`e9602e4`), criterion 6 and the runner's half of criterion 7 in PR #106 (`761f56a`), each by `moxywolf-agent[bot]` after Dorian's approving review at the head, recorded by `record-release` as `agent_merge_autonomous`. Criteria 2 and 7's settings were made by Dorian on 2 October 2026: the `goal-holdout` environment limited to `main`; the `goal-branches` ruleset on `goal/*` (no deletion or force push, pull requests only, `goal-envelope` from GitHub Actions, an empty bypass list, creation allowed); and `main-gate` requiring `tests`, `goal-envelope`, `goal-tests` and `goal-holdout` from GitHub Actions, branches up to date, stale approvals dismissed and code-owner review. Criterion 9's tests that need those rulesets to refuse something run in B-e.

**Links introduced:** the goal-branch-to-main link. A reader of `main` assumes a goal's changes arrived together, reviewed as a whole, after its goal tests and holdout passed at one head. That holds because the goal branch reaches `main` only through a pull request with its own cross-vendor review and required checks.

1. `/gstack-goal <id>` runs `goal_brief.py verify <id>`, records the result, and opens `goal/<id>` from `main`.
2. A ruleset on `goal/*` requires a pull request and the envelope check (GO-004.1), and blocks direct and force pushes. The bot can't bypass it.
3. The runner refuses to start when the builder's model family drafted the goal tests or read them (GO-002.1 and .4), or when `agent_token.py permissions` shows a secrets permission (GO-002.3). Items run in `PLAN.md`'s order, one at a time, each through the existing `/gstack-build` loop with its pull request targeting `goal/<id>`. Every item is built, even after the goal tests pass, up to Max items; the finish waits for the last one, because an item the tests don't reach is still part of the approved goal. The cross-vendor review rules stay as they are. The cap is the brief's Max review rounds, never more than three, so DR-113's escalation after three blocking rounds still holds.
4. After each item merges to `goal/<id>`, the runner runs the goal tests at the new head and records which pass. An `invariant` test that fails, or an `outcome` test that passed and now fails, stops the run.
5. Every goal check (`goal-envelope`, `goal-tests`, `goal-holdout`) is a workflow under `.github/workflows/` that runs on `pull_request_target`, so its definition comes from the base branch, and it checks out the enforcement scripts and `goals/<id>/` from `main` by SHA, never from the pull request's head. `goal-holdout` runs only on pull requests into `main`, so it runs in the `goal-holdout` environment. Each job resolves the pull request's head SHA and GitHub's merge candidate when it starts, evaluates that candidate, re-reads the pull request before publishing, and publishes nothing if the head or the base moved, because the new push or base change starts a fresh run. It publishes its verdict as a check run on the head SHA through the workflow's own `GITHUB_TOKEN`, so the check comes from GitHub Actions. Each ruleset that requires one of them names GitHub Actions as its only accepted source, so a status the bot posts under the same name doesn't satisfy it, and a verdict on one head never satisfies another. Of the three, only `goal-tests` and `goal-holdout` execute candidate code, and only under the isolation in GO-002.3. Every token the runner and builders use during a goal run is minted by `agent_token.py` with `contents` and `pull_requests` only and no `workflows` permission, so GitHub refuses any push that adds or changes a workflow file, and no workflow of the agent's can run before a merge-time check sees it.
6. When every goal test passes, the runner opens the pull request from `goal/<id>` to `main`. The `goal-holdout` workflow (under `.github/`, so a `CODEOWNERS` path) runs on that pull request's merge candidate, checks the secret against `holdout.sha256` from `main`, runs it, and reports the check `goal-holdout`. The ruleset requires that check from the GitHub Actions source only, so a status the bot posts under the same name doesn't satisfy it. A holdout failure stops the run and is reported as a possible reward hack. The check names the failing holdout tests and their error lines, never the holdout's source, so the failure can be diagnosed; that holdout is then spent.
7. The pull request into `main` gets a fresh cross-vendor review of the whole diff at its head with coverage `checked`, `tests` green, the envelope check over the whole diff against `main`'s copy of the brief, a required check that runs the goal tests from `main`'s copy of `goals/<id>/`, a passing `goal-holdout` check, and, while the staged approvals in this objective's introduction apply, Dorian's approving review at the head, which GitHub enforces as code-owner review because the pull request carries the goal's run record, `goal-runs/<id>/RESULT.md`, and `goal-runs/` is a `CODEOWNERS` path. `goal-envelope` fails a goal pull request into `main` that doesn't carry it, so that review can't be skipped by leaving the record out. It's outside `goals/<id>/`, so writing it doesn't change the approved tree. After the holdout's preconditions are met, the runner opens one finalize pull request into `goal/<id>` whose only change is that file; the envelope check allows `goal-runs/<id>/RESULT.md` in a pull request that changes nothing else, and no other `CODEOWNERS` path anywhere. The ruleset dismisses stale approvals on push, so his approval holds only at the head he approved, and a withdrawn approval blocks the merge. Every required check on this pull request runs on GitHub's merge candidate, and the ruleset requires the branch to be up to date with `main` before merging, so a move of `main` reruns them. It merges and is recorded through `record-release` like any other change.
8. A run ends in one of three outcomes, written to the run record and the final digest: `complete` (merged to `main`), `stopped` (with the stop condition that fired), or `exhausted` (the plan or Max items ran out with goal tests still failing). Only `complete` is success.
9. Tests with stub builds cover: a plan run in order; a regression that stops the run; a holdout failure; an exhausted plan; a direct push to `goal/<id>` (refused by the ruleset, quoted in B-e); an item pull request that replaces `goal_envelope.py` or its workflow with an unconditional pass, which still fails the check because `main`'s copy runs; a goal tree that changed mid-run (stopped); a builder from the drafting or the reading family (refused); a token with a secrets permission (refused); a holdout whose hash differs from `holdout.sha256` (the `goal-holdout` job fails); candidate code that tries to read the holdout file, read the environment or open a network connection during the holdout job, each of which fails; a push after the holdout passed, which reruns `goal-holdout`; Dorian's approval given after the checks, withdrawn after them, and followed by a push, each read by GitHub at the head; the whole finish, from the last item through the finalize pull request to his approval and the merge; a verdict published for one head that doesn't satisfy the next; a `goal-holdout` status posted by the bot, which doesn't satisfy the ruleset (quoted in B-e); `main` moving after the checks passed, which blocks the merge until they rerun.
10. A goal run's token reads what a private repository hides (amended 2026-10-05). `agent_token.py --goal-run` mints contents and pull_requests write plus checks, actions, environments and deployments read, because GitHub hides all four from a token without those scopes, and a run reads them to decide a merge (GO-005.4) and to see what a push would set off (GO-005.5). Read scopes only: the token still can't write a workflow or touch a secret, and `goal_run.py start` still refuses one that can. `goal_run.py` takes the governed repository from its checkout's origin, so a run in another repository needs no `--repo`.
11. `goal_run.py merged` runs the goal tests at the merged head in the same container, with the candidate's dependencies installed, that `goal-tests` uses in CI and baseline uses at start (added 2026-10-05). On the bare host the worktree had no `node_modules`, so no-em-dashes-2's invariant, which runs the candidate's `tsc`, failed after item 1 and stopped the run on a regression that the container didn't reproduce. `test_goal_run.py` checks that `merged` judges through `goal_brief.sandboxed`.
12. Delivery is the completion rule for a goal in delivery mode (proposed 2026-10-26 with goal `gstack-delivery-through-merge`; accepted on that goal's approval). Each item keeps a durable record of its phase (preflight, build, verify, review, prepare release, merge, record and synchronize, finish), its candidate head and base, context digest, source bindings, test receipts, review chain, required checks, PR identity, release handoff, merge record and synchronization result, referencing existing records rather than copying them. The runner resumes from that record after a session or tool restart and revalidates head, base, authority, budget, context and prerequisites before advancing. A timed-out push or merge is inspected remotely before any retry; a merge that succeeded but was not recorded is recorded, never merged again. The goal is `complete` only when every planned item and its declared release companion are merged and recorded, goal tests and holdout rules pass, and every named required working copy is synchronized; otherwise the record reports the applicable phase, blocker or `sync_pending` naming the checkout and reason. Routine in-scope failures (a failed test, a demonstrated review blocker, a missing required version bump, outdated evidence after an authorized fix, an ordinary sync from `main`) are the agent's to fix within the original limits; a checkpoint is history, not a prohibition on corrective commits.

### GO-004 — The envelope is enforced outside the agent, and the runner stops spend near the cap

**Status:** done. Criteria 1, 3 and 4 merged in PR #102 (`d3147ef`) and criterion 2, as rewritten on Dorian's decision, in PR #103 (`df0bb1c`), each by `moxywolf-agent[bot]` after his approving review at the head, recorded by `record-release` as `agent_merge_autonomous`. Reviews `20261001-210512-a89e96e-ngr7o8bw` and `20261001-214159-92f8389-40wbjpfc` (codex), both `fixes_verified` in 2 rounds. The runner's read of the ledger before each step is GO-003's `goal_run.py next`.

1. `goal_envelope.py` runs as a required check on every pull request into a `goal/` branch and on the goal pull request into `main`. It fails when any changed file is outside the brief's Allowed paths or matches a `CODEOWNERS` path, and names each file. It reads the brief from `main`, never from the branch, and examines only the files changed by commits on the head that aren't on `main`, so commits that arrive from `main` aren't the goal's changes. Keeping `goal/<id>` up to date is a pull request into it from a branch at `main`'s tip plus one empty commit, which carries no goal-authored changes and so passes (the empty commit gives the head its own check suite; a head that is `main`'s own commit has its checks filed with `main`'s push, where they don't count); a conflict in that merge stops the run. A sync pull request merges under DR-113's authority once the envelope check finds no goal-authored commits and `goal-tests` is green, without a new cross-vendor review, because every commit it carries was already reviewed into `main`. `goal_run.py may --action merge --sync 1` allows it only when its head changes no file against `main` and its checks are green for that pull request. A sync pull request with any goal-authored commit is an item pull request and follows GO-005.4. The goal never moves the marketplace's top-level version, because `main` moves that line too and every sync would conflict: `goal_envelope.py` fails a goal-authored change to it, `version_bump.py` measures a goal-branch range from where it left `main` and asks no top-level move in goal mode (a pull request into or out of `goal/<id>`, a push to one, or a goal's merge into `main`), and after `complete` the agent opens the release bump, a pull request into `main` that moves only that line.
2. The runner counts every model call in a goal run. The dispatcher appends one line per call to the run's spend ledger, the file `GSTACK_GOAL_LEDGER` names: the provider, the cost the provider reported for that call, and the transport. A transport with no metered key (a subscription CLI), and a metered call whose provider reports no cost, count against Max calls, and `goal_brief.py check` refuses a dollar budget for a subscription CLI, which could never be reached. Before each step the runner reads the ledger with `goal_spend.py status` and stops at 80% of any provider's entry in Provider budgets, of the Spend cap, or of Max calls, and escalates (GO-006). A charge to a provider with no budget, a ledger it can't read, or a ledger write that fails stops the run. The run record shows the totals. The provider's own credit limit on the team key stays the backstop outside the agent.
3. `goals/<id>/HALT`, when present on `main`, stops the runner before the next step. Only a person can add it to `main`, because `goals/` is a `CODEOWNERS` path.
4. Rolling back a merged goal is a revert pull request of its merge commit through the normal gated path, never a reset of `main`. The final digest names the merge commit and the command that opens that revert.
5. Tests cover: a changed file outside Allowed paths; a changed `CODEOWNERS` path; a brief edited on the branch (ignored, `main`'s copy used); `main` advancing with a change to a `CODEOWNERS` path, synced into `goal/<id>` through the sync pull request, which passes the envelope check, while the same change authored on an item branch fails; a run at Max calls (stopped); Provider budgets that sum past the Spend cap (refused by `check`); spend split across two providers, each stopping at its own budget; a call whose provider reports no cost (counted against Max calls); a charge to a provider with no budget (stopped); an unreadable ledger (stopped); a ledger write that fails (raised past the dispatcher's own error handling); a halt file present (stopped).
6. Cumulative review and time limits (proposed 2026-10-26 with goal `gstack-delivery-through-merge`; accepted on that goal's approval). Per delivered change, one initial independent review and at most two repair-verification passes in total, counting replacement review IDs; a fourth reservation is refused. Recovery stops after two repair cycles for a recurring failing check without a resolved cause. Approved active-time limits persist across restarts and are not reset by a new session, branch, item name or review ID; waiting on CI or an owner is reported separately with a bounded deadline. Failed dispatches and calls count under the existing spend policy. An unreadable gate, budget or authority record blocks further action and is never read as a pass.

### GO-005 — A judgment call is a record, and code decides who makes it

**Status:** done (PRs #107 and #108). Criteria 1 to 3 are in `plugins/gstack-execution/scripts/goal_calls.py` and `goal_run.py`'s `call`, `council` and `answer` (PR #107). Criteria 4 and 5 are in `goal_guard.py` and `goal_run.py`'s `act` and `may`, with criterion 6's tests in `test_goal_guard.py`. Readings recorded: item branches are `build/*`, the names `/gstack-build` gives them; “deployment integrations the repository reports” are the environments GitHub lists and the environments its deployments name, so an app that deploys on a push without reporting an environment isn't seen; a workflow counts as triggering when any of `push`, `pull_request`, `pull_request_target`, `workflow_run` or `merge_group` appears in it, which refuses on the safe side; the repository's own checks are `tests.yml` and the three goal workflows at the content pinned in `goal_guard.CHECKS`, which moves only in a change Dorian approves, and a test fails CI while a pin and its workflow disagree; the brief's Stop conditions can name only a workflow whose top-level `on:` block is exactly `push` with `branches: [main]`, comments aside, or an environment; an item merge also needs `tests`, `goal-envelope` and `goal-tests` green at its head, read from GitHub's check runs, and the goal checks count only when their newest run names, in its `external_id`, this pull request, the goal branch and its current base; the recheck reads the workflows on `main`, because the envelope keeps `.github/` out of every goal change; an item merge reads its review record from `GSTACK_PEER_REVIEW_DIR`. On a private repository the goal-run token can't list environments, which needs Actions read, so `start` refuses there until the app and the goal-run token carry it (review F3, deferred; this repository is public).

**Links introduced:** the decision-to-rule link. A reader of a decision record assumes the action type it names is what the change does. That holds as far as the code detectors in criterion 2 reach, and anything they can't place goes to Dorian.

1. Every judgment call the agent makes during a run is written by code to the run record: the question, the options, who framed them, the action type, the votes and dissent, the commit IDs it affects.
2. Action types are a fixed list, and code assigns them from the change, not from the agent's label: `in_envelope_code` (every file inside Allowed paths, no change to a dependency manifest or lockfile, no new environment variable name, no new hostname in code or config), `dependency` (a manifest or lockfile changed), `external` (a new hostname or environment variable name), `goal_brief` (anything under `goals/`), and `unclassified` (anything else).
3. Only `in_envelope_code` is decided by the council: two models from families other than the builder's, one told to argue against the leading option, with the options framed by a model other than the one proposing. A split council escalates. Every other type goes to Dorian, and the run waits.
4. Every action the runner or a builder executes during a goal run is also checked against the existing action classes in `governance.py` (`GRANT_CLASSES`, `ONE_SHOT_ONLY`, `check_egress`) with a ledger made for the goal. That ledger grants `vcs.push` to `goal/<id>` and item branches, `pr.open` into `goal/<id>` and once from `goal/<id>` into `main`, `review.send_code` and `external.model_call` to the reviewers the run uses, and `net.connect` to their hosts; it never grants `merge`, which stays in `ONE_SHOT_ONLY`. An item pull request merges into `goal/<id>` under DR-113's authority, exactly as a change merges into `main` today: pinned to its head, after a clean cross-vendor review at that head with coverage `checked` and the goal checks green, recorded by `record-release`. That authority covers `goal/<id>` targets only; the final merge into `main` follows GO-003.7. Any other action, including one that reaches an external system through a hostname or variable that already exists, is refused at the agent's step and sent to Dorian. That refusal is attention, not containment (*Constraints*). Containment is GitHub's: the `goal/*` and `main` rulesets, the app's permissions, and criterion 5. An `in_envelope_code` type never authorizes an action the ledger doesn't grant.
5. Pushes and merges are assessed for their effects before a run starts. `goal_runner.py` refuses to start when any workflow under `.github/workflows/`, or any deployment integration the repository reports, triggers on a push to `goal/*` or item branches, on a pull request into `goal/*` or `main`, or on a push to `main`, and does anything beyond the repository's own checks and the goal checks, and it names each one. A goal whose merge into `main` would trigger a deployment needs that deployment named in its brief's Stop conditions as Dorian's call, or the run doesn't start. The runner repeats this check against the current workflows and integrations before every push, pull request it opens and merge, and stops if anything it would trigger differs from the start of the run.
6. Tests cover: an in-envelope call recorded and decided; a workflow that deploys on a push to any branch, and one that deploys on a pull request, each of which stops the run from starting; a deploying workflow added to `main` after the run started, which stops the run before its next push; an item merge into `goal/<id>` after a clean review, allowed, and the same path aimed at `main`, refused; the final pull request into `main` opened under the one-time grant, and a second one refused; a call to an existing external host that isn't a reviewer, refused by the ledger although the change is typed `in_envelope_code`; a lockfile change, a new hostname and a new environment variable name each typed by code and sent to Dorian whatever the agent labels them; an unclassified call that waits; a split council that escalates.
7. The repository's own checks are pinned in that repository (amended 2026-10-05). The three goal workflows are the same file in every governed repository, so `goal_guard.GOAL_WORKFLOWS` pins them. Each repository pins its own checks, its `tests` workflow first, in `.github/goal-checks.json` on its `main` as `{"checks": {"<workflow file>": "<sha256>"}}`. That's a `CODEOWNERS` path, so a pin moves only in a change Dorian approves. A pin file that doesn't parse, pins a goal workflow, or pins anything but a workflow file to a lowercase SHA-256 is a problem that stops the run. A test fails CI while any pin and its workflow disagree.
8. A preview deploy that a hosting integration builds on every pushed branch (Vercel's, say) is outside what GitHub reports, so criterion 5 can't see it. It's allowed only when the project's Preview environment variables hold no production credential: test or sandbox values only, so code that hasn't been reviewed yet has nothing worth taking. Turning previews off isn't required. `/init-goal-mode` says so for any repository with such an integration. Before the first goal there, Dorian confirms it, or the agent establishes it from the host's API without reading a value. Until one of those happens it's reported unverified, and no goal runs there (amended 2026-10-05).

### GO-006 — Dorian hears from the run at milestones and on escalation, and nothing else

**Status:** review; human merge pending. Built in `plugins/gstack-execution/scripts/goal_run.py` (`notify`, `digest`, `escalate`, `outbox`, `acknowledge`, `holdout-failed`, `ack`) with criterion 4's tests in `test_goal_digest.py`. Readings recorded: “sends” means the runner prints each new message with whatever command produced it and the agent relays it to Dorian as written; the run record keeps it whether or not he saw it. Findings carry no category, so code reads “the same finding category” as a blocking finding in the same file, by its bound path in the review record, on two consecutive items. “What the agent is least sure of” is the agent's own statement, required on every `merged`. A close call is one with dissent. The escalations on the review cap, spend, a goal-test regression and a holdout failure end the run; one on a call that's Dorian's waits through the call; a goal-ledger refusal, a resync the envelope refuses and the repeated-file escalation hold the run until he acknowledges it in his words, and every action refuses while one is open; spend is checked before every action as well as between items. An item pull request that `goal-envelope` refuses is criterion 2's change outside the envelope: `goal_run.py refused` escalates it with the check's own output quoted, and it holds the run like a goal-ledger refusal. Added after the adversarial pilot's scripted attempt, PR #131, was refused by the check with no path into the run record.

1. The runner sends one digest per finished item and one at the end, answering three questions: which calls were close, what the agent is least sure of, and what changed against the brief. Dissent is shown by default. A digest asks nothing of him and gates nothing.
2. It escalates at once on: the review cap reached on an item, 80% of the Spend cap or Max calls, a change outside the envelope, the same finding category on two consecutive items, a goal-test regression, a holdout failure, or a GO-005 call that's Dorian's. An escalation waits for him.
3. Every digest and escalation is written into the run record as well as sent, so a missed message is still on file.
4. A test covers each escalation trigger in criterion 2 with a stub run, and checks that a digest doesn't block the next item.
5. In delivery mode the run escalates only for a changed outcome or scope, missing authority or access, exhausted limits, a mandatory owner gate, or a substantive blocker with no allowed recovery (proposed 2026-10-26 with goal `gstack-delivery-through-merge`; accepted on that goal's approval). It never asks whether to continue an already authorized step, and sends one consolidated report per escalation: intended outcome, exact blocker and evidence, recovery attempted, remaining budget, smallest decision needed.

### GO-007 — `/gstack-goal-new` drafts a goal, and no one family writes the tests, explains them and builds against them

**Status:** review. Opened 2026-10-04 after goal mode's acceptance run: each of the five goals so far was drafted by hand-written one-off scripts, the same steps every time.

**Links introduced:** none. The drafter, the reader and the builder were already three families (GO-002.1, GO-002.4, GO-003.3); this item makes the drafting path one command instead of a fresh script per goal.

1. `/gstack-goal-new <id> "<outcome>"` drafts `goals/<id>/` on a branch from `origin/main`. The agent writes `GOAL.md` and `PLAN.md`. It agrees the outcome with Dorian in one exchange and asks him nothing else before the pull request.
2. `goal_new.py tests <goal dir>` has the gpt family, through Codex read-only, draft exactly the test files the brief's Goal tests name. Each file carries that family's `drafted-by` line, whatever line the drafter wrote. A reply missing a named file, or adding one, writes nothing and exits 1. A drafted test is never edited by hand: a wrong one is fixed in the brief or plan and drafted again.
3. `goal_new.py holdout <goal dir>` has the same family draft the holdout against the plan and the pre-mortem. The holdout goes to `~/.goal-holdouts/<id>.py` (folder 700, file 600; `GSTACK_GOAL_HOLDOUTS` overrides), refused when that folder is inside the repository. Only its SHA-256 enters `holdout.sha256`. The command prints the file's path, the secret name `GOAL_<ID>_HOLDOUT` and the environment, never the holdout. A reply that doesn't parse or has no `class Holdout` writes nothing and exits 1. It stays there until the run's finalize step carries it into the goal pull request (GO-002.8).
4. `goal_new.py read <goal dir>` has the gemini family, through OpenRouter, read every goal test back in plain English. It prints the goal pull request's `## Plain-English reading` section with its `Read by:` line. It refuses a reading that misses a test, a model below the reader's floor, and tests whose drafting family is missing or is the reader's.
5. `goal_brief.py check` and `baseline` still decide whether the folder is a goal. The command opens the pull request adding `goals/<id>/` alone and tells Dorian, in one message, the link, that it needs his approval, and where the holdout secret goes.
6. A test covers, with stub drafters: tests written under the drafter's family; a missing file refused with nothing written; a brief with no goal tests; the holdout outside the repository at 600, hashed, its source absent from the output; a holdout folder inside the repository refused; an unparseable holdout and one with no class refused; a reading covering every test with its reader named; a reading that misses a test, one below the floor, and tests drafted by the reader's family, each refused.
7. product-orchestrator's sprint protocol routes a build plan of two or more items to `/gstack-goal-new`, then `/gstack-goal` once Dorian approves the goal, and a single item to `/gstack-build`. It names `/init-goal-mode` as the step that says whether a repository can run goals (amended 2026-10-04).

### GO-008 — Before goal mode runs in a repository, a check says what that repository lacks

**Status:** review. Opened 2026-10-04: goal mode is proven on this repository only. Everything that enforces it lives in the governed repository's own `main` under Dorian's code-owner review. Another repository needs the same pieces before `/gstack-goal` can run there, and nothing said which it had.

**Links introduced:** none. The check reads; it changes nothing in either repository.

1. `goal_ready.py <owner>/<repo>`, run from a moxywolf-plugins checkout under `agent_token.py exec --repo <owner>/<repo>`, reads the repository through the GitHub API and reports seven requirements: the bot can see it, read its check runs and its deployments, and its default branch is `main`; `DESIGN.md` on `main` has at least one objective heading; `CODEOWNERS` names Dorian on every gate path this repository's `CODEOWNERS` lists under `/.github/`, `/goals/`, `/goal-runs/` and the goal-mode scripts; `.github/goal-checks.json` pins the repository's own checks, and they and the three goal workflows match their pins byte for byte (GO-005.7); every non-test file under `plugins/gstack-execution/scripts/` is the same git blob as the checkout's `HEAD`; `main`'s rules require code-owner review and the `tests`, `goal-envelope`, `goal-tests` and `goal-holdout` checks, and block force pushes and deletion; the `goal-holdout` environment deploys from `main` only.
2. Each requirement prints `ready`, `missing` or `unknown` (the API refused) with what it examined, then a count line. It exits 0 only when all seven are ready. Unknown is not ready. A repository the token can't see stops after its first line.
3. `/init-goal-mode <owner>/<repo>` in project-init runs the check and turns each gap into who acts. Dorian installs the app, sets the ruleset and the environment, and approves any `CODEOWNERS` change. The agent writes `DESIGN.md` through `/gstack-design-doc`. It changes nothing on its own.
4. Installing goal mode in another repository is not built here, and the check says so by its own result. The per-repository pin is GO-005.7 (2026-10-05). The sandbox runs a Node candidate as GO-002.7 sets out (2026-10-05). Copying the workflows and scripts into a repository is that repository's own onboarding pull request, which Dorian approves; it's never done by hand.
5. A test covers, against a stub API built from this checkout: a repository set up like this one, ready on all seven; each requirement failing alone; a refused call reported as unknown; an invisible repository stopping after one line.

### GO-010 — A goal runs in any repository, with no onboarding

**Status:** building. Declared 2026-10-05 on Dorian's instruction, after a goal on SAMS was refused for lacking setup: "Fix it NOW so that I can test it on ANY ... REPO".

**Links introduced:** none. Results print as JSON and go into the goal pull request and the run's RESULT.md.

Goal mode ran only in a repository whose own `main` carried its gates: workflows, gate CODEOWNERS, a ruleset, an environment. Each new repository cost Dorian admin steps and approvals before its first goal, and SAMS, a pnpm monorepo, couldn't install in the sandbox at all. In local mode the checks GitHub would run in CI run on the Release Owner's Mac, in the same containers, from the moxywolf-plugins checkout, and Dorian's approvals of the goal pull request and the final pull request are the gate. In such a repository GitHub enforces nothing extra, which the pull request says.

1. `goal_local.py check <repo-dir> <id> <head> [--holdout <file>] [--goal-dir <dir>]`, run from a moxywolf-plugins checkout, runs every goal test against `<head>` in the goal-tests container with the verdict scripts from that checkout, never from the repository under test, and with the goal folder from the repository's `origin/main` or `--goal-dir`; with `--holdout` it checks the file against `holdout.sha256` and runs it in the holdout container; it prints one JSON object and exits 0 only when every goal test, the holdout when given and the envelope passed.
2. A pnpm workspace installs in its own networked container from its manifests only (every tracked `package.json`, `pnpm-lock.yaml`, `pnpm-workspace.yaml`, `.npmrc`), frozen lockfile, scripts off, hoisted, with workspace links re-pointed at the sandbox's copy of the candidate; a symlinked manifest is refused; an npm repository uses the existing install.
3. The envelope applies one rule in every repository: it refuses any goal commit touching `.github/`, `goals/` or `goal-runs/`, any path the repository's own CODEOWNERS assigns an owner, or any file outside the brief's Allowed paths, and any change to the marketplace's top-level version. No CODEOWNERS file, complete or partial, switches the fixed gate off; local mode commits no holdout, so there is no finalize commit to except.
4. `goal_local.py baseline <repo-dir> <id> --goal-dir <dir>` fails unless every invariant passes and every outcome test fails on `origin/main`, and `goal_local.py brief <repo-dir> <id>` runs `goal_brief.py check` with that repository's DESIGN.md and CODEOWNERS.
5. `/gstack-goal-new` and `/gstack-goal` describe local mode as the path for a repository `goal_ready.py` reports as not set up, asking Dorian for nothing beyond the goal approval and the final approval.
6. `test_goal_local.py` covers the local envelope (fixed gate, an ordinary or sentinel-only CODEOWNERS, the marketplace version), the manifest selection with a symlink refused, a dangling lockfile link, the link re-pointing, a baseline outcome that didn't run, and a failed check still printing JSON; the SAMS run is recorded in `docs/evidence/go-010-sams-2026-10-05.md`: its pnpm install, two of its unit test files passing in the sandbox, and a baseline with the invariant passing and the outcome failing.
7. `plugins/gstack-execution/.claude-plugin/plugin.json` moves to 0.77.0 and the marketplace to 1.115.0.

### GO-011 — Dorian co-authors the goal, the goal merges itself, and he tests usability before Production

**Status:** building. Declared 2026-10-24 on Dorian's instruction: "I want to set the goal. Co-Author the PR. And then once everything has been fully committed and e2e tests are done, I want to do usability tests." His answers the same day: the brief is co-authored as a live doc before its pull request; the usability test sits before Production, not before `main`; it's a guided task sheet; and the agent triages what he finds. This amends the paragraph above that kept his review on the goal branch's pull request into `main` until two or three clean goals: his decision replaces that wait.

**Links introduced:** a live doc per goal draft (Claude Docs) and a usability sheet per finished goal (an Artifact page with a shared store). Both links go to Dorian in the conversation; their contents also land in `goal-runs/<id>/`.

Dorian decides three things per goal: its outcome (by co-authoring the brief), its approval (the goal pull request), and whether it's usable (the sheet, before Production). The agent asks him nothing else for a goal: no item, sync, finalize or final pull request waits on him. The safety stops GO-005 and GO-006 define (spend at its stop, a change outside the envelope, a ledger refusal, a judgment call the council splits on or that isn't in-envelope code) still hold the run and reach him; when one means the goal is wrong, the agent proposes the next goal rather than an override.

1. `/gstack-goal-new` drafts the brief as a live doc Dorian can edit and comment on before anything is committed: his outcome in his words, the never-do's, the scenarios and the plan items. It opens the goal pull request only after he says the draft is right. The commit carries `Co-Authored-By: Dorian C <dorianc@moxywolf.com>`, and the pull request says the brief is co-authored. The tests, holdout and reading are still drafted by other families (GO-002, GO-007). His approving review of that pull request starts the run.
2. The goal branch's pull request into `main` merges as the bot, with no review by Dorian, once `goal-envelope`, `goal-tests`, `goal-holdout` and `tests` are green and a fresh whole-goal cross-vendor review has no blocking findings. `CODEOWNERS` keeps `goal-runs/README.md` and stops owning a goal's own `goal-runs/<id>/`; the holdout is still checked against `goals/<id>/holdout.sha256`, which stays owned. `goal_run.py propose` says so in the pull request, and `goal_run.py may --action merge --resource main` grants the merge only then: the review pinned to the head where the goal tests passed, or after a sync from `main` to a fresh review of the new head without its holdout; the review's packet (`packet.json`, which `plugins/gstack-execution/scripts/peer_review.py` writes at `open` with `review_base` set to the base and keeps through fix rounds) names every item and starts where `main` and the goal head meet.
3. A failed `goal-holdout` never comes to Dorian as an override. The run ends, and the agent drafts the next goal with him (criterion 1), with what failed as its input.
4. After the goal merges, `/gstack-usability <id>` writes a usability sheet: the Preview of the merged commit, and five to eight tasks taken from the goal's Scenarios, each written as something a person does ("find which skills automate incident response"). For each task Dorian marks worked, confusing or broken and can add a note; the sheet stores his answers where the agent reads them back. The agent sends him the link once and doesn't nag.
5. The agent triages his answers. Wording, layout, styling, a broken link or an accessibility fix that doesn't change what the product does is fixed through `/gstack-build` (cross-vendor review, no goal), verified on the Preview, and handed back as a re-test sheet with only those tasks. Anything that changes what the product does becomes the next goal, drafted with him (criterion 1).
6. Production means the product's own domain (for wfa-web, wfa.opencontrols.ai). A host that deploys on every merge into `main` (Vercel's Git integration) also serves the host's own address (on Vercel, `<project>-<suffix>.vercel.app`, which Vercel leaves public); that isn't Production. Dorian decided both on 2026-10-24: "keep as is" over a release branch or testing before the merge, and "leave it public" for the vercel.app address. The brief's Stop conditions name attaching the domain as his call, and `/gstack-usability` checks the domain isn't attached before sending the sheet. Production and domains stay his call, asked once, when every task's latest mark across the goal's sheets is worked (unanswered isn't, and a task sent to the next goal keeps its mark) or he says to ship. Nothing else about the goal comes to him, apart from the GO-005 and GO-006 safety stops named above, which hold the run.
7. `plugins/gstack-execution/.claude-plugin/plugin.json` moves to 0.93.0 and the marketplace to 1.147.0.
8. The goal approval carries goal-level merge authority (proposed 2026-10-26 with goal `gstack-delivery-through-merge`; accepted on that goal's approval, in the approving person's own words). It covers implementation, verification, review, routine in-scope fixes, branch pushes, PR creation and gated merges for that goal, including its declared release-companion PR into `main`, which the runner tracks, reviews, merges as the app and records before declaring the goal complete; inside those boundaries the workflow asks for no per-PR merge permission. It waives nothing: CODEOWNERS, repository rules, deployment restrictions, data-use permissions and any separate required human approval still block the relevant action until satisfied. Scoped, freshness-checked context from the graph-context runtime (`graph_context.py`, attest, pointer bindings, preflight refusal) is how read-only graph workers receive evidence; no worker receives merge authority.

### Dissent from the design review

The two-reviewer loop (XE-031's first live run: Codex on gpt-6-astra and Gemini 3.8 Flash, writer Claude) ran five rounds and stopped at its cap. Reviewers raised 36 findings across the two loops (16 in the first, 20 in the second); every one was accepted and fixed. The two below were raised in the last round, so their fixes haven't been reviewed. Log: `Taskade/Team Plugins/06 – Engineering/DESIGN-REVIEW-LOG-2026-10-01.md`. A first loop ended `incomplete` when Gemini 3.1 Pro used its whole 16,000-token output on reasoning; its sixteen findings carried into the second.

| Finding | Held by | Raised in round | Writer's position |
|---|---|---|---|
| `R5-r1-1` (material): GO-003.7 has no permitted path for adding RESULT.md. The final PR's source is goal/<id>, so RESULT.md must first enter that branch. Direct pushes are forbidden by GO-003.2, while a PR adding it is rejected by GO-004.1: the protected-path exception applies only to the final PR into main. GO-002.5 also stops any run that changes the goal folder. Consequently, the run cannot construct the PR that is supposed to require Dorian's approval. Specify a gated finalization path and narrowly scoped RESULT.md exceptions, preserving the approved charter, plan and tests. Test the complete sequence from finishing the last item through adding the record and obtaining final approval. | codex | 5 | accepted: Run record moves to goal-runs/<id>/RESULT.md, outside the approved tree; one finalize PR into goal/<id> may change only that file; goal-runs/ joins CODEOWNERS; end-to-end finish test added. Applied after the last review round, so unreviewed. |
| `R5-r1-2` (material): GO-003.5–7 does not specify how pull_request_target results become required checks bound to the tested merge candidate. That event runs in the default-branch context; checking out another commit does not by itself bind the emitted check to that commit. GitHub requires checks on the applicable head or test-merge SHA. Specify candidate SHA resolution, trusted publication of each verdict against that SHA, and rejection of stale results after either parent changes. Extend B-e to demonstrate that a passing candidate actually satisfies the ruleset and that its result cannot satisfy a different candidate. See [workflow event context](https://docs.github.com/en/actions/reference/workflows-and-actions/events-that-trigger-workflows#pull_request_target) and [required-check SHA requirements](https://docs.github.com/en/pull-requests/how-tos/merge-and-close-pull-requests/troubleshooting-required-status-checks). | codex | 5 | accepted: Each goal check resolves head and merge candidate at start, re-reads before publishing, publishes nothing on drift, and posts a check run on the head SHA via GITHUB_TOKEN; B-e shows a verdict satisfies its own head and not the next. Applied after the last review round, so unreviewed. |

### GO-012 — One release relationship, and the delivery record is written by the run that uses it

**Status:** planned. Approved by Dorian 2026-10-27 ("Approve GO-012 as drafted"). Drafted 2026-10-27 from `/gstack-investigate` on the delivery path at `5b1a6d8` (PR #267), after goals `gstack-delivery-through-merge` 1 to 5 and `gstack-delivery-completion` 1 to 3 each failed their own review on it.

**Links introduced:** the delivery-record link (`goal-runs/<id>/state.json` `delivery`, written by `goal_run.py start` and read by every later phase); the release-relationship link (one function, `goal_guard.release_relationship`, that every merge-shaped decision calls, so the recorded finalized head means the same thing in `may merge`, `next_action`, `complete` and `record-release`).

**Root cause (two findings, both confirmed in the code at `5b1a6d8`).**

1. **Nothing creates the delivery record.** `state["delivery"]` is assigned only inside `goal_run.resume` (goal_run.py:1097, :1132), and `resume` refuses when the record is absent (:1063). `start`, `finalize`, `propose` and `complete` never write it. So in a real run `complete` reaches line 763 and ends the goal `complete` at the main merge, before the release record, the companion and the Mac sync. The end-to-end demonstration passed only because `test_goal_run.delivery_state` writes the record by hand (test_goal_run.py:524-540). Criterion GO-003.12 is therefore unbuilt behind a passing test (EV-001: a check over input it did not examine).

2. **The reviewed-head/merged-head relationship has five homes.** Whether a goal pull request may merge, and whether its merge counts, is decided separately in `goal_guard.final_merge_allowed` (:208-214), `goal_run.final_review_pinned` (:418), `goal_run.complete` (:747), `goal_delivery.next_action` (:202-214, "Repair 6") and `peer_review.cmd_record_release` (`finalized_relation`, "Repair 6" again). Each review round found two of the five disagreeing, each repair added a sixth place that restates the rule, and the next round found the new disagreement. In the chain from `87e7b81` to `5b1a6d8` (46 commits, 8 repair commits), these three files grew by 1,056 lines and 16 were removed. This is XE-008 ("a fact with two homes drifts") in the goal layer, and it is why the rounds do not converge.

**Why a goal cannot fix it.** A goal run merges through this path, so a defect in the path blocks its own delivery; the finding then becomes a new goal, which runs through the same path. The change below is one ordinary `/gstack-build` change to `main` with one cross-vendor review, under the existing DR-113 rule; none of the files is a `CODEOWNERS` path except where noted.

1. `goal_run.py start` writes `state["delivery"]` (schema_version 1) from the approval it already verifies: `approval` = the goal PR, Dorian's approving review id, its commit id and his login; `max_review_rounds` = the brief's Max review rounds; `changes` = `final` (target `main`, repository from the checkout's origin) and, when the brief declares one under `## Release companion`, the companion (target `main`); plan items are not duplicated here, because the run already keeps them in `items`, `done` and `results` and the whole-goal review scope checks them (amended 2026-10-27 with Dorian's approval, before any code); `release_companion` from `## Release companion` (`marketplace` or `none`) and `required_checkouts` from `## Required checkouts` (one line each: `name path host branch`), both empty when the brief has no such section. `resume` on a run started before this change still refuses with its current message.
2. Each existing phase binds its own facts into the record as it goes: `finalized_head` and `final_pr` in `propose`; the whole-goal review id at `may merge main`; the merge sha from GitHub's observation in `resume`. No new phase and no second place for any of these; the record references the existing run fields and review ids.
3. One function, `goal_guard.release_relationship(state, head, pr, pinned, scope_problems)`, returns `{"ok": bool, "finalized_head", "tested_head", "problems"}`: the pull request is the recorded goal pull request, its head is `state["finalized_head"]`, and the review is pinned to the tested head without its holdout and covers the whole goal (`goal_run.final_review_pinned` and `final_review_scope`, passed in because `goal_guard` can't import `goal_run`). `final_merge_allowed`, `goal_delivery.next_action` (the `ch["final"]` branch), `goal_run.complete` and `peer_review.cmd_record_release` call it and keep no comparison of their own. A test asserts by source that the strings `finalized_head` and `tested head` are compared in that one function only.
4. `goal_run.py complete` no longer ends the run when a delivery record exists: it records the merge and returns the delivery status, and `resume` (or the same session continuing) carries the release record, the companion and the sync to `complete`. Only `resume`'s end at `delivery_status == "complete"` writes outcome `complete`.
5. The four blockers from review `20261027-1549…` of `5b1a6d8` are folded in, from the desktop session's unpushed `e9a3055` where they match this design: a release re-check compares like rounds only; an item review is never accepted as the whole-goal review (`final_review_scope` is called from `release_relationship`); a checkout's repository comes from its remote, and a declared `repo` that disagrees blocks; a Git read that times out reports `sync_pending`.
6. The end-to-end demonstration test starts from `goal_run.start` with no hand-written delivery record and runs finalize, propose, merge, record-release, companion and sync against the fixture GitHub. It fails on `main`'s tree today at `complete` and passes with this change.
7. `test_goal_run` keeps a case that a record `resume` would accept is refused when `start` did not write it.
8. gstack-execution gets a minor bump and a README Version History line; the marketplace entry and top-level version move (CI-002).

**Not in this item.** The fourteen embedded-instruction follow-ups the reviews listed; the preserved candidate branches, which stay as they are; moving the review verdict into a GitHub-required check (named as later work in the DR-113 constraint).

**Evidence for the reviewer.** The two root-cause citations above, with line numbers at `5b1a6d8`; the chain statistics (`git diff --stat 87e7b81~1..5b1a6d8` on the three files; `git log --oneline 87e7b81..5b1a6d8 | grep -ciE 'repair|fix'` = 8 of 46); and the demonstration test's red run on `main` before the change.

## Twelfth objective: the Chief of Staff

Opened 2026-10-06. That morning the marketplace took in eight department agents from headcount (`cbrock84/headcount` at `98d1c17`, MIT): finance, people, it-operations, security, pmo, operations, corporate-strategy and customer-experience. Each one is good inside its own remit. None of them knows the others exist, and nobody owns routing an ask between them, running them on a schedule or keeping track of what they decided. Dorian asked for a Chief of Staff to do that job, built to three outside sources: Anthropic’s Claude Managed Agents article, the Agent SDK cookbook’s Chief of Staff agent, and AI Maker’s “AI Chief of Staff setup” post (all read 2026-10-06).

The three sources describe the same parts: a context file, specialist subagents, slash commands, an audience-specific output style, an audit trail, a plan before acting, memory, approval rules and a scheduled routine. Here’s where each part lives in this plugin:

| Source part | Lives here as |
|---|---|
| CLAUDE.md company context | one vault file, read on every run (DR-085: company memory is written, not remembered) |
| `.claude/agents/` subagents | the department agents already in this marketplace, called by name and not copied: eight at CS-001, ten from CS-004 |
| `.claude/commands/` | the plugin’s skills: `/cos`, `/cos-brief`, `/cos-review` |
| Executive output style | the decision memo format in the operating manual |
| Approval rules and scoped permissions | the authority table (autonomous / proposes / escalates), ported from headcount’s `agent-hierarchy` |
| Self-evaluation against outcomes | the return contract, checked before any result reaches Dorian |
| Audit hooks and tracing | the decision log (see the constraint below for why this isn’t a hook yet) |
| Scheduled deployment | a weekday Cowork scheduled task that runs `/cos-brief` |

**Settled decisions**

- **The runtime is a Cowork plugin** (Dorian, 2026-10-06). The layout follows the cookbook’s, so moving to Managed Agents later is a port, not a rewrite. That port isn’t an item yet.
- **The Chief of Staff runs in the main session, not as a subagent.** Claude Code doesn’t give a subagent the Agent tool, so a Chief of Staff defined as a subagent couldn’t dispatch anyone. The cookbook does the same thing: its orchestrator is the main agent and holds the Task tool.
- **Authority is wider autonomy** (Dorian, 2026-10-06).
  - Autonomous: read Jira MOXY, session handoffs, calendar, mail, Drive and the vault. Dispatch any department for analysis. File or comment on Jira MOXY tickets, with the project label declared by the ticket’s project. Write deliverables into a Taskade project’s numbered folders.
  - Proposes: edits to vault shared knowledge, and changes to a ticket someone else owns. Code goes through `/gstack-build`.
  - Escalates: anything that leaves MoxyWolf (email, Slack, posts, invites to other people), any spend (credits, purchases, subscriptions), a conflict between departments, and any blocking finding from security.
- **Headcount’s `executive` plugin contributes ideas only** (Dorian, 2026-10-06). The authority levels and the rule that producer and auditor are never the same agent are re-expressed in our words. No file is copied. When departments disagree, the Chief of Staff doesn’t arbitrate, because Dorian is the CEO. It writes him a decision memo.
- **Security reviews, and nobody reviews their own work.** When a department’s output touches identity, secrets, customer data or an external integration, the Chief of Staff sends that output to security before it reaches Dorian. Security’s blocking findings stand, as its agent definition already says. Amended 2026-10-07 (Dorian): a disagreement that involves sales-department or finance doesn’t wait for that review, and one that involves finance is run by the outside bookkeeper. CS-004 criterion 8 carries the rule.
- **No audit hook in this objective.** In a cloud session a plugin hook writes to the container, and the container is reclaimed, so the log it kept would disappear with it. Cowork’s transcript is the trace for now. The decision log is written by the skill, which is a recalled record, not a captured one, and the operating manual says so. Moving to Managed Agents tracing is the upgrade path.
- **Reuse before writing.** `/cos-brief` builds on `daily-briefings:morning-brief` and doesn’t re-read the sources that skill already reads.

### CS-001 — The Chief of Staff routes an ask to the departments that own it

**Status:** review. Merged unreviewed: merged to `main` in `2ef7868` (PR #186, head `7d0ba15`) on 6 October 2026 by `moxywolf-agent[bot]` on Dorian’s instruction, recorded on the pull request. His exception, in his words: “Merge, log the tool gap: Merge 7d0ba15 as merged_unreviewed, with your exception quoted in your words. Then open a new design item for peer_review.py so files a criterion names are packed first and never get crowded out by large changed files. That fix goes through its own loop.” Two reviews by `openrouter-gpt` (gpt-6-astra). `20261006-202034-4e2ec3a-qsez89ny` found two blocking defects in the decision-log instructions, both fixed (`46fbd34`, `d3b7707`), and ended `rounds_exhausted`. `20261006-211544-7d0ba15-ywbtet8r` at `7d0ba15` raised no blocking finding and left criteria 4, 9 and 10 unmet only because the review surface withheld the files they name (XE-035). CI `tests` was green at the merged head (run 37532195182). Records: `Taskade/Team Plugins/06 – Engineering/peer-reviews/`.

**Links introduced:** the context card’s vault path (`MoxyWolf Vault/_Shared Knowledge/Operating Norms/chief-of-staff-context.md`), which re-resolves by reading the file; the decision log’s path (`Taskade/<project>/00 – Project Hub/chief-of-staff-log.md`), which re-resolves the same way; and the eight agent names in the roster, which re-resolve against `plugins/<name>/agents/<name>.md`.

1. A new plugin, `plugins/chief-of-staff`, carries `plugin.json`, a README with a Version History, and a marketplace entry, and its version is the same in all three places.
2. The skill `chief-of-staff` is the operating manual. It covers the role, the reading order (context card, then the project’s instructions, then the ask), the roster, the authority table above, the return contract and the decision memo format.
3. The roster names each department agent with a one-line remit, and it settles the three overlaps.
   - Incidents: a security event goes to security, and an operational outage goes to operations.
   - Identity: account creation and removal go to it-operations, and access policy and access review go to security.
   - Program management: across projects goes to pmo, and inside one process or vendor relationship goes to operations.
4. The roster also names what the eight don’t cover, and where that work goes instead: legal, sales, marketing, product and engineering go to the installed `legal:`, `sales:`, `marketing:`, `product-orchestrator:` and `/gstack-build`. Nothing in the roster is routed to an agent that doesn’t exist. The eight roster entries resolve to `plugins/finance/agents/finance.md`, `plugins/people/agents/people.md`, `plugins/it-operations/agents/it-operations.md`, `plugins/security/agents/security.md`, `plugins/pmo/agents/pmo.md`, `plugins/operations/agents/operations.md`, `plugins/corporate-strategy/agents/corporate-strategy.md` and `plugins/customer-experience/agents/customer-experience.md`. Product and engineering resolve to `plugins/product-orchestrator/.claude-plugin/plugin.json` and `plugins/gstack-execution/commands/gstack-build.md`. Legal, sales and marketing come from other marketplaces and aren’t in this repository.
5. `/cos <ask>` classifies the ask and names the departments it’s sending it to before dispatching. Departments that don’t depend on each other run in parallel. Each dispatch carries the context card’s path and the ask’s success criteria.
6. A department’s result is accepted only when it carries the four fields the department agents promise (the answer, the skills loaded, the sources cited and the open questions). A result missing a field is sent back once, and if it’s still incomplete it’s reported as incomplete, never filled in by the Chief of Staff.
7. Anything in the escalates class stops and reaches Dorian as a decision memo. The memo has a one-paragraph summary, each department’s position in its own words, the options with a recommendation, and what happens if he does nothing. It follows his voice rules.
8. Every `/cos` run appends one entry to the decision log: the date, the ask, the departments dispatched, the outcome (done, proposed or escalated), and any decision Dorian made.
9. The context card exists in the vault, holds MoxyWolf’s entities, products, tools, team and standing rules, and is the only place those facts live. The plugin points at it and doesn’t copy it.
10. A stdlib test (`plugins/chief-of-staff/tests/test_roster.py`) fails when the roster names an agent with no `plugins/<name>/agents/<name>.md`, or when a department agent in this marketplace is missing from the roster. It reports what it examined, and an empty roster FAILS (EV-001). `plugins/gstack-execution/scripts/run_all_tests.py` discovers it.

### CS-002 — The morning brief dispatches the day

**Status:** building.

**Links introduced:** the scheduled task’s ID, which re-resolves through `list_triggers`.

1. `/cos-brief` reads `daily-briefings:morning-brief`’s output, plus Jira MOXY across all project labels, and turns it into three lists: dispatches it ran, proposals waiting on Dorian, and escalations. Each list leads with its count.
2. The autonomous dispatches have run, and their results are in the brief, before the brief reaches Dorian.
3. A weekday scheduled task runs `/cos-brief` at a time Dorian sets, and the brief says which approval setting the task got.
4. A brief with nothing in it still says what it read and how many items each source returned. Saying that nothing came back doesn’t count as a clean day (EV-001).

### CS-003 — The weekly scorecard keeps the roster honest

**Status:** building.

**Links introduced:** none beyond CS-001’s decision log.

1. `/cos-review` reads the past week of the decision log and grades each department Keep, Tune or Cut, with the log entries behind each grade.
2. A Tune names the change, either to the context card or to the routing, and proposes it. It isn’t applied without Dorian’s approval.
3. A department with no dispatches that week is reported as unused, not graded.

Missing or stale links are reported as gaps, not treated as authorization.

Existing `sales:` and `marketing:` skills remain specialist dependencies from other marketplaces. Distinct package names prevent shadowing those namespaces. The eight existing department agents and their packages remain unchanged. Historical CS-001 delivery evidence remains as recorded.

Add `plugins/sales-department/agents/sales-department.md` and `plugins/marketing-department/agents/marketing-department.md`, each with a department-specific description and a head skill loaded first: `sales-department:head-of-sales` and `marketing-department:head-of-marketing`. Add `plugins/sales-department/skills/head-of-sales/SKILL.md` and `plugins/marketing-department/skills/head-of-marketing/SKILL.md`. These are operating and routing instructions, not copies of existing specialist skills.
2. Each package carries `.claude-plugin/plugin.json`, `README.md` with Version History, `GOVERNANCE.md`, and head-skill `references/sources.md`. Register both in `.claude-plugin/marketplace.json` with matching local manifest versions. Bump the changed Chief of Staff version consistently in `plugins/chief-of-staff/.claude-plugin/plugin.json`, its marketplace entry and README. The marketplace’s top-level version moves (CI-002).
